# Saint Nicaise <note>This work, published anonymously by Johann Joachim Christoph Bode, is a seminal piece of 18th-century Masonic literature. While it claims to be a translation from French, it was actually an original German critique of the mystical "Strict Observance" and "Rectified Scottish Rite" branches of Freemasonry.</note>

## Foreword.

I must inform the reader in a few words about the fortunate coincidence <note>original: "glücklichen Ohngefähr," implying a stroke of luck or a providential accident</note> by which this important collection of letters came into my hands. A few months ago, I was making a journey from Hamburg to Paris. On the third day, I crossed the Weser <term>Weser [a major river in northwestern Germany]</term> at Laase. At Diebesnau <note>likely a historical spelling for a village or coaching station near the Weser river</note>, our traveling party was further joined by a man who appeared to be about fifty years old. This man’s outward appearance was very engaging. He spoke little; but what he said was instructive and entertaining, and in general, he revealed himself to be a man of noble character and

I conversed with him the most, and the same affinity <note>original: "Sympathie," referring to a natural rapport or mutual attraction of character</note> that drew me to him, drew him also to me. My business necessitated that I remain a few days in Osnabrück <term>Osnabrück [a city in Lower Saxony, Germany]</term>. He did the same, as he wished to be able to make the entire journey with me. On this occasion, I learned from him that he intended to travel further than Paris. As I likewise had to travel soon to Lyon and Marseille, I already resolved <note>original: "Anschlag," here meaning a plan or intention</note> within myself to continue my further travels in such good company.

After we had stayed three days in Osnabrück, we traveled to Münster <term>Münster [a major city in Westphalia]</term>. There he already felt ill; he nevertheless continued his journey until Dorsten <note>original: "Dürsten," likely referring to the town of Dorsten in North Rhine-Westphalia</note>, where he was seized by such a violent illness that it was impossible for him to travel any further. ## Foreword.

...whereupon various things in cipher followed again.

There was still a note <note>original: "Zedel," an archaic spelling of "Zettel"</note> in the pocketbook that might have provided me with more information: *Messrs. Tourton and Bauer will have letters for Monsieur Vaulin from his very humble servant Rochefort.* <note>original French: "Mess. Tourton et Bauer auront des Lettres pour Monsieur Vaulin de son très humble serviteur Rochefort"</note>

# Foreword.

## No. I.

might easily have received some news through the efforts of the aforementioned office <note>original: "Comtoir," referring to the banking house of Tourton and Bauer mentioned previously</note>. Since no one has come forward, they belong to the public. Since the letters concern the Order of Freemasons <term>Freemasons [a fraternal organization that uses secret rituals and symbols to teach moral and philosophical lessons]</term>, that body might well have the first claim to them. Because I do not have the good fortune to belong to this society, and thus do not know to whom I could deliver them—and as they also contain much that is very interesting for the general public—I believe I owe it to the profane <note>original: "profanen"; here used in the Masonic sense to mean the uninitiated or the general public outside the order</note> public, to which I belong and which concerns me more closely, to share these letters with them. Paris, the 31st of October, 1783.

->Jean Baptiste Leuillard.<-

### No. 1.

Yes, my dear friend, I confess it, that I am your **debtor** <term>debtor [here used metaphorically to describe someone who owes a correspondence or a promised reply]</term>, and even more, that it was my **intention** never to pay my debt. Had you not found such a zealous **advocate** in my brother, I would still persist in my resolution. What then should I do now, since he makes it a **matter of conscience** for me if I do not reply? He writes to me:

> Give to him who asks of you; and from him who wants to borrow from you, do not turn away. <note>original Latin: "Qui petit à te, da ei; et volenti mutuari à te, ne avertaris." This is a quote from the Gospel of Matthew 5:42, often used to emphasize the duty of charity and responsiveness.</note>

Under such circumstances, I must then indeed answer. Do not believe, however, my dear friend, that the intimate **friendship** I harbor for you has ever been extinguished in my heart, simply because I did not answer your three letters from last year at all, and your latest one from this year still required the **assistance** of my brother. No, the same one, who

<meta>This page continues the letter from the previous page, where the author began explaining to his friend why he had remained silent for so long and how his brother eventually persuaded him to write back.</meta>

the same person I have always been toward you, I remain still. But when I decided to take flight to this place, I made a resolution—one I deemed highly necessary for my peace of mind—to henceforth sever all other bonds. To this resolution I have remained faithful until now, and my brother has been the only person with whom I have occasionally maintained a correspondence. I have chosen this little nest as my place of refuge. Do not destroy it by telling others where it is; instead, view my brother's trust as a sign that he wished to use you to fill my place once again.

You marvel at my resolution, and certainly, you are right to do so. When we first made each other's acquaintance, we were different people, and we would have laughed in the face of anyone who had told me that I would finish the remainder of my days in this lonely corner, and that I should once more read the <term>Breviary</term><note>original: "Breviair"; a liturgical book of the Catholic Church containing the daily psalms, hymns, and prayers to be recited by those in holy orders</note> before the <term>Crucifix</term><note>original: "Crucifix"; a representation of a cross with the figure of Jesus Christ on it, central to Catholic devotion</note>. wanted to break each other's necks, they were now the best of friends and as tame as lambs. Initially, they had intended to enter the Benedictine Order <note>original: "Benedictiner Orden"; a Catholic religious order of monks following the Rule of Saint Benedict</note> at St. Remi <note>The Abbey of Saint-Remi in Reims, France, a major center of learning and monastic life</note>; however, they were now traveling to Italy to carry out their intentions there. It is nevertheless remarkable that we, who were formerly companions <note>original: "Gefehrten"</note> in a completely different way of life, have now chosen almost the same path toward a future life. I am certain, my dear friend, you would do the same if the principles <note>original: "Grundsätze"</note> in which you were raised permitted you to choose this path.

## For my Gaston.

When this package is delivered to you, my beloved child, by the faithful friend of your father and your own, I shall already be an inhabitant of a better world, where dangers and downfall are no longer to be feared. Look upon this then as a secure guide <note>The original German "Leitfaden" literally means a "leading thread," an allusion to the thread used to navigate the mythological Labyrinth.</note> that I give you to find your way out of many wrong paths. It may please Providence to preserve me long enough that I may instruct you myself in all that you read here. In that case, it shall serve to remind your spirit from time to time of all these events, should they perhaps escape your memory. See here, in all instances, the image of your father presented to you in his history, so that you—warned and instructed by his example—may avoid the diversions that could plunge you into ruin; and on the contrary, may walk with firmness the paths that either I, or my friend, will show you, in order to fulfill your destiny and—

Such is man when he is in a state of **passion** <note>The author uses "passion" (Leidenschaft) in the 18th-century sense to describe intense worldly desires or impulses that cloud a person's ability to see reality clearly.</note>. Everything appears to his eyes differently than it truly is. Once the passion is satisfied, only then do objects regain their previous form. 

I am firmly convinced that I would have even then turned back, if it had been possible and if I had not felt ashamed before my cousin, who was present; so difficult was it for me to remove myself from a place where I had enjoyed so many gentle, innocent, and quiet joys, and where I left behind so many virtuous friends.

**Vocabulary:** Mother, Approval, Directors, Taskmasters, Passions, Virtuous friends

Soon thereafter I traveled in the company of my cousin to my maternal uncle, who lived in Abbeville on a **prebend** <term>Pfründe: a stipend or income granted to a clergyman, usually drawn from the lands or tithes of a cathedral or collegiate church.</term>. This man loved me with such extraordinary tenderness, as if I had been his own child. All the moods and eccentricities that were peculiar to him ceased as soon as he saw me; and whoever wished to obtain something from him was certain of the granting of his wish if they only turned to me. 

I do not know what intentions he might have had for me; but when I came to him in **uniform** <note>The narrator is likely wearing the uniform of a junior officer or a specific military academy, signaling his definitive break from the path of the priesthood.</note> to take my leave of him, bright tears flowed from his cheeks. He spoke much to me about the step I had taken, and sought to represent my choice to me from as disadvantageous a side as was possible. 

I certainly sought to refute him with every conceivable reason; but my **philosophy** <note>In this context, "philosophy" refers to the narrator's personal logic and the rationalizations he used to justify his career change.</note> was a weak dwarf against his, and moved by his reasons—or rather by his tender sympathy for my fate—I would have gladly turned back again, if I could have [done so] with honor—

At that time, I saw for the first time the **Marquis de Lernay** <note>Gabriel-Auguste de Monteynard, Marquis de Lernay, a French officer credited with introducing "higher degree" Freemasonry to Berlin around 1758.</note>, through whom our **Masonry** <term>Masonry: The practices and principles of the Freemasons.</term> was later brought to **Germany**. Thereafter, our march toward Germany took place. These Masonic degrees were a rarity among us at that time. 

Anyone who was a **Scots Master of Saint Andrew** <note>original: "Ecosais von St. André." A prestigious "high degree" in the French Masonic system of the time.</note> was still regarded as a great Mason in those days. It was because of this that I later received the position of **Junior Warden** <term>Junior Warden: (original: "zweyten Aufsehers") One of the three principal officers of a Masonic lodge, responsible for the supervision of the members during their periods of rest.</term> in the **traveling lodge** <term>Traveling Lodge: (original: "Ambulante") A military Masonic lodge that followed a specific regiment on campaign rather than meeting in a fixed building.</term> of the **Vicomte de Grave**—a post to which neither my age (for I was not yet twenty-one) nor my **regularity** <note>Referring to the strict adherence to Masonic rules and the time required to serve in lower offices before promotion.</note> would otherwise have given me a claim.

On our march to **Germany**, I had the misfortune of being seized at **Châlons** <note>Châlons-en-Champagne, a city in the Grand Est region of France.</note> by such a violent fever that I was absolutely compelled to stay behind. My illness lasted over four weeks, and even when I had recovered, the doctors deemed it necessary for me to spend a few more weeks there before joining the **army**, in order to fully restore my strength. This

—this <note>The word "dieser" (this) began on the previous page as "die-".</note> small delay had the most significant consequences for the coming days of my life, and perhaps I would never have become as happy and as unhappy as I later became, had this not occurred. I am far from believing that **Providence** <term>Providence: (original: "Vorsehung") The protective care of God or nature as a spiritual power; a central concept in 18th-century philosophy.</term> works any evil so that something good may arise from it. But she, who oversees the whole, permits a lesser evil to happen, and likely uses our own transgressions to secure us against others.

During my stay at **Châlons** <note>Châlons-en-Champagne, a city in the Marne department of France, often a staging point for troops moving east.</note>, I became acquainted with a young man who called himself the **Chevalier de la Villette** <note>Chevalier (Knight) was a title of nobility, often used by the younger sons of aristocratic families.</note>. His mother was the widow of an officer and lived on a **pension** <term>Pension: A regular payment made by the government or the King, in this case to support the family of a deceased officer.</term> given to her by the King to reward the merits of her husband, who had perished in **Germany** <note>original: "in Deutschland geblieben" — literally "remained in Germany," a common euphemism for dying in battle.</note> during the last war <note>Given the timeline, this likely refers to the War of the Austrian Succession (1740–1748).</note>. I soon became a familiar guest in his mother’s house; upon her son’s recommendation, she granted me permission to visit her as often as I wished. This permission became a source of much **sorrow** <gloss>original: "Bekümmernisse" (troubles or grief)</gloss> for all of us. Madame de la Villet—

I now took counsel with myself on how I should reveal my passion to **Eloise**. At one moment I was inclined to reveal myself to the mother, at another to the brother; but hardly had I made a resolution when I again found reasons that advised me to abandon it. In general, my whole situation—as a young, unknown officer who was on the verge of departing for the army—left me almost no hope at all. Madame von Beaubois, to whom I was to send my letters. It was a most singular mixture of emotions that stirred within my soul upon reading her letter. I would have given a whole world for her now. It was my blood that received growth and nourishment from her blood—my strength, the "other self" of both me and **Eloise**. I looked forward with rapture to the moment when I would fold this child of our love in my arms. On the other hand, I was in the utmost distress on **Eloise’s** account, and cursed my unhappy fate, which prevented me from traveling immediately to **Châlons**, throwing myself at the feet of **Madame de la Villette**, confessing our transgression to her, and asking for her daughter's hand.

While I was still struggling with these thoughts, my cousin stepped into my room to invite me to a <term>pleasure outing</term> <note>original: "Partie de Plaisir"; a social excursion or party common among the upper classes</note> that he, **Virieux**, and some other officers had planned for that day in a garden. The confusion in which I found myself could not possibly remain hidden from my cousin for long. Upon his vehement urging to reveal to him the causes of my sorrow—which, as he told me, was provoking very inconsistent and, in part, disadvantageous judgments regarding my honor among our friends—I found myself compelled to reveal the whole story to him. I read him the letter I had just received, which was still lying on the table, and asked him to support me with his advice. He listened to me until the end: but how great was my dismay when, at the conclusion of my tale, he burst into loud laughter! "Is it nothing more than that, you poor fool?" he said to me. I firmly believe that, amidst the great corruption of morals <note>original: "Verderben der Sitten"</note> that prevailed among our young men in the army, I would have become extremely dissolute; but by now it was deeply inscribed in my soul that I no longer belonged to myself. Thus, my own excess <note>The narrator suggests that his singular, overwhelming devotion to his beloved acted as a shield against the common vices of military camp life, such as drinking or gambling</note> had to serve as a means of protection against other excesses.

If I had formerly learned—under the guidance of a certain vain self-love (for it was certainly no longer virtue)—to become master of myself in situations where my friends gave free rein to all their passions, it was now my love for *Eloise* <note>original: "Eloysen"; likely a reference to a specific woman or a literary ideal, perhaps influenced by the popularity of Rousseau’s "Julie, or the New Heloise" at the time</note> that held me back. Alongside this, I sought out all manner of things that could occupy and distract me: I learned *German* and *English*, and my talent for languages allowed me to achieve a fair degree of proficiency in a short time. I understood everything I read and expressed myself well enough. I say well enough; for a *Frenchman* will never reach such perfection in the German language that one would not notice the foreigner in him—

We had in the army a **traveling lodge** <note>original: "Loge ambülante"; these were mobile Masonic lodges that moved with military regiments</note> that was chartered by the **Prince of Condé**. My old zeal for advancing in its **mysteries** awoke within me anew; and although I was not so fortunate as to see much light <note>A common Masonic metaphor for spiritual or philosophical enlightenment</note> before me, I nevertheless arrived at several concepts during my investigations which I later realized were not so entirely far from the **truth**. At the very least, I differed from all my friends in the **Order** in that I did not regard it as merely a matter of pleasure, but suspected **mysteries** of importance within the **Order**. What most confirmed me in this was the acquaintance I had the opportunity to make after the **Battle of Warburg** <note>Fought on July 31, 1760; a significant conflict where the Allied forces defeated the French</note> with a Scotsman named **Fraser**.

On the day after this **encounter** <note>original: "Treffen"; referring to the Battle of Warburg</note> had taken place, I visited the battlefield with some of my friends. However much I had already grown accustomed to the most horrific sights during the short time I had spent at war, this scene nevertheless left me completely unnerved. Blood and corpses covered the field. The majority of them had been stripped naked—partly by our soldiers, and partly by the peasants of the neighboring villages. The latter generally sought to compensate themselves in this way for the losses they were forced to endure through **requisitions** <term>Lieferungen|The mandatory supply of food, fodder, and labor that civilians were forced to provide to occupying or passing armies</term> and the devastation of their fields. These bodies were now awaiting the order for their **hasty burial** <term>Einscharrung|A rudimentary burial, often in mass graves, without the usual religious or military honors</term>.

A number of peasants were already occupied with this task. I distanced myself somewhat from my friends so that I could surrender myself all the more undisturbed to my thoughts and feelings. Some peasants, who were busy clearing away the corpses, drew my attention. From among the other half-clothed or naked dead, they pulled out a body which I—by its clothing, however much it had become unrecognizable from blood and dust—

now, since she had been completely restored to health, she was earnestly pressing to bring her to **Saint Denis** <note>original: "Sainct Denys"; likely referring to the Maison royale de Saint-Louis at Saint-Cyr or a similar convent school near the Abbey of Saint-Denis, where young noblewomen were educated and often shielded from suitors</note>, and that all the attempts and excuses that had been employed until then were in vain. In the embarrassment in which I found myself, I sought counsel from Mr. Fraser, without however confessing to him the **misstep** <note>original: "Fehltritt"; a polite 18th-century euphemism for a moral lapse or an illicit romantic affair</note> of which I and Eloise had made ourselves guilty. We reveal ourselves to the unworthy, and when we find ourselves deceived by them, we become reserved toward others who do not deserve our mistrust, and thereby often harm ourselves. on, I was completely reconciled with the whole concept of chivalry <note>German: *Chevalerie*; the knightly code and romantic literature associated with it</note>. My Claude made for a magnificent Sancho Panza <note>The loyal, practical squire to Don Quixote in Miguel de Cervantes' novel</note>. I mention this because I firmly believe that this episode provided a slight occasion for me to plunge later, and with all the more ease, into those Masonic follies <note>German: *maurerischen Thorheiten*; a reference to the narrator's future involvement in the rituals and secrets of Freemasonry, which he now looks back upon as foolishness</note> of which I shall speak in the future. Eloyse felt the happiness of our reunion just as much as I did, and I believe to an even greater degree. She was, in general, a person of very lively emotions. My entire conduct toward her, and my efforts to keep her, were sufficient proof of the intensity of my passion: this

I therefore wanted to postpone this ceremony <note>The marriage ceremony Eloise had requested on the previous page</note> until our arrival in Germany, and I hurried as much as Eloise’s circumstances would allow—as she was not accustomed to such continuous and arduous travel—to reach the borders.

We arrived one evening at Bouchain <note>A fortified town in northern France, which at the time sat near the border of the Spanish/Austrian Netherlands</note>. Since we had nearly reached the border, I wished that we would not stop there, but rather travel on immediately. ...finally been informed of the whole truth of the Order. <note>This completes the opening thought: the author is describing the moment he felt he truly understood the deeper purpose of the Masonic organization.</note>

Since my history from this time forward stands in the closest connection with the Order, and the most prominent of my experiences originated from there, I must explain myself somewhat more circumstantially regarding this. <term>Masonry: Freemasonry</term> is actually a symbolic order. Just as it was with the <term>mysteries of the ancient world: Refers to ancient initiatory rites such as the Eleusinian or Egyptian mysteries, which 18th-century Masons believed were the ancestors of their own rituals</term>—where the lesser mysteries were preliminary initiations into the greater ones—so it is also with the Masonic Order. Actual Masonry is a preparation for another matter and, in a sense, a painting <note>original: "Gemälde". In this context, it refers to a symbolic representation or "Tracing Board" used to teach moral lessons through visual allegory.</note>, of whose true nature and meaning one is only accurately and completely informed once one has traversed the entire <term>Masonic career: The sequence of degrees or ranks through which a member progresses</term> and has reached the fortunate goal from which one can oversee everything. These were the concepts I formed for myself about the Order during the earliest days after my entry into it. This is a thought that everyone must immediately arrive at who even slightly surveys the Order, and

Among various other acquaintances I made in the **Lodge**, I also became acquainted with the **Abbé de Lüze**. The local **Masons**, who all belonged to the faction I had abandoned, regarded me with cold indifference, and it proved impossible for me to gain an audience with the **Magus** <note>Referring to Schrepfer, who claimed magical powers</note>.

Shortly thereafter, **Schrepfer** returned to **Leipzig**, and I followed him. Since he kept a public house <note>Schrepfer operated a coffee house, which served as a front for his occult gatherings and séances</note>, it was easier for me to make his acquaintance there. I was indeed so fortunate—or rather, so unfortunate—as to be present for several of his **operations** <term>Operationen: A term used in alchemy and ritual magic to describe the performance of a specific ceremony or experiment</term>.

Some of these displays had every outward appearance of fraud and sleight of hand <term>Taschenspielerey: Conjuring or "pocket-play"; 18th-century stage magic used to deceive an audience</term>. In the room, mirrors had been fixed to various walls. Ritual circles were drawn and candles were lit; a thick vapor was produced using an unpleasant incense <term>Rauchwerk: Incense or fumigants used in rituals, often intended to create a somber atmosphere or provide a medium for "ghostly" projections</term>. All manner of preparations were underway...

The shapes were more like a mass of mist molded into a human figure than an actual human being; nonetheless, the face, with all its features—which were terrible to behold—was quite recognizable. The figures were entirely grayish-brown in color; yet the clothing was recognizable and almost the same as the **Knight Templar** depicted in his domestic habit in **du Puy** <note>Referring to Pierre Dupuy’s "Histoire de l'Ordre Militaire des Templiers," a well-known historical work on the Templars often used as a reference for costumes in Masonic rituals</note>. They spoke, but very indistinctly and hollowly, almost like people speaking who lack the roof of their mouth.

[...questions that I] received as an answer, were admonitions <term>Admonitions (original: "Ermahnungen"): Formal warnings or earnest advice, often given by a religious authority to encourage repentance.</term> to examine my entire life closely and not to hide the truth through lies and excuses. Although I constantly flattered myself with the hope of soon undergoing an interrogation <term>Interrogation (original: "Verhör"): The formal judicial questioning used by the Inquisition to extract a confession.</term>, it was continually postponed from one time to another. I had already spent four months in this abode of misery <note>The phrase "Aufenthalt des Jammers" is a poetic, dramatic way of describing the wretched conditions of his prison cell.</note> without the slightest hope of progress. 

Boredom plagued me in an unspeakable way; I had nothing to occupy myself with but my own thoughts and mental images, and these offered no comfort. However, as sorrowful as this situation was, it offered me the great advantage of allowing me to review my entire life. I recognized the wrongs and criminal acts of which I was guilty—failings I had fallen into solely because of the flawed ideas I held regarding the Order <note>In the context of 18th-century German literature, "the Order" (der Orden) often refers to a secret society, such as the Illuminati or the Freemasons, which were frequently depicted as having mysterious and sometimes dangerous influence.</note>—and I made a firm resolution to renounce all of them entirely, if only I were lucky enough to escape the danger in which I now found myself.

They had even relented in the persecution of other **Masons** <term>Masons (Maurer)</term> who did not belong to the **Hundian system** <note>The Rite of Strict Observance, founded by Baron Karl Gotthelf von Hund, which dominated German Masonry with its claims of Templar origins and strict hierarchical "despotism."</note>. Under these circumstances, a Baron named **Gugomos** <note>Baron Gottlieb von Gugomos (1742–1781) was a famous Masonic adventurer and "Cagliostro-like" figure who claimed to have been initiated into the "true" secrets of the Knights Templar in Cyprus.</note> had also arisen in Upper Germany. What one would never have believed actually happened: a large portion allowed themselves to be deceived, and measured...

And finally, to make the Babylonian confusion <note>original: "babylonische Verwirrung"; a biblical reference to the Tower of Babel, used here to describe the chaotic state of the Masonic order where no one spoke the same language or shared the same goals.</note> even greater, a new sect emerged in Germany that called themselves the **Gold and Rosicrucians** <term>Gold and Rosicrucians (Gold- und Rosenkreuzer): An 18th-century mystical fraternity that claimed ancient origins and practiced alchemy, exerting significant influence over high-ranking Freemasons.</term>. They passed themselves off as the only genuine and true Freemasons and had no lesser goal than to discover the **Philosopher’s Stone** <term>Philosopher’s Stone (Stein der Weisen): A legendary substance in alchemy believed to be capable of turning base metals into gold and providing the "elixir of life."</term>. As much as people had previously striven to keep anything called "secret sciences" away from the Order, they now threw themselves into them with equal eagerness. The Order dissolved into a multitude of small factions, all of which aimed to uncover its "secrets." What was most striking was that not a single one of these factions was satisfied with the others; indeed, they seemed to hate one another with all their might.

This sickness—of which our Masons previously knew nothing, or by which only exceptionally few had been infected—had now also reached France. When I returned from Italy, I already found many among my former acquaintances who were talking about I-know-not-what kinds of secrets.

From that time on, I observed that the Chevalier <note>original: "Chevalier"; a title for a knight or a member of the lower nobility, frequently used in Masonic circles to denote rank.</note> visited me even more frequently than before, and did everything possible to gain even more of my favor. I did not hold this increased pressing against him, even if the advantages he hoped to gain from me were the cause of it. For a completely selfless friendship is perhaps something that does not exist at all; and even where it appears most selfless, we still always have some regard for ourselves. I had, after all, done the same thing, and I certainly would not have sought the favor of Mr. Shirley, the Abbot <term>Abbate: An Italian title for a cleric, often used in the 18th century for men in minor orders who moved in social and intellectual circles.</term> Gabrieli, and others so diligently, if I had not expected certain advantages from their company.

### No. 6.

It has been very pleasing to me, my worthy friend, that my story has been an entertaining read for you. But it has truly surprised me that you, as it seems to me, wish to consider it more as a novel <note>original: "Roman"; at the time, many "Masonic novels" were written as fictional allegories to teach moral lessons.</note> that I devised to make the various **Masonic** <term>wrong paths</term> <note>original: "Abwege"; refers to the various controversial or mystical splinter groups that deviated from mainstream Freemasonry.</note> clear to my son, rather than as a real history. No, my dear friend, everything is truly true, and happened to me exactly as I have written it down; and I have even omitted many small circumstances that did not serve my purpose. I have therefore also passed over all those incidents that had no connection with the **Order** <note>The Masonic Order.</note>: these lay outside my field of vision. If I had wanted to mention all of these, and thus write my entire history in the most literal sense, I would have produced a very voluminous book. That my life can be called, in a certain sense, a **Masonic** life, I still attribute to my stay in Ger-

...it is a stroke of luck for it <note>Referring to the Masonic Order.</note> that it is not known to the world. Here, I do not agree with you. You are confusing the Order itself with the mass of Freemasons one encounters in the world, and even regarding these latter ones, what you say does not hold true. I would like to single out from the **Masonic folk** <note>original: "Maurervolk"; a somewhat dismissive term for the general membership.</note> those who belong to the **English Constitution** <term>English Constitution</term> <note>This refers to the "Moderns" or the original Grand Lodge of England, which emphasized morality and simple ritual over the elaborate mystical "High Degrees" popular in Continental Europe.</note>, or such Masons as we still had in France in the years from 1750 until approximately 1778; I would find nothing to criticize in these people. It is indeed true that their insights do not extend at all beyond what is called the **secrets of mere Masonry** <note>The basic three degrees of Apprentice, Fellow Craft, and Master Mason.</note>. They know no other reason to give for everything that occurs among them than that "it must be so," that it is thus in accordance with the Constitution; and where that is insufficient, they take refuge in an explanation based on **morality** <note>original: "Moral"; many Masons of this period interpreted the symbols strictly as metaphors for ethical behavior.</note>. But regardless of that, these are perhaps the best among the countless multitude of Masons. The spirit of self-interest, of pride, of factions, and of persecutions, which constitutes the main trait in others, is to them

If brotherly love is still to be found anywhere among ordinary <term>Masons</term> <note>The general membership of the fraternity.</note>, it is certainly among these people. They suspect that the <term>Order</term> <note>The Masonic brotherhood.</note> contains deeper mysteries, but they believe these are too distant and unattainable for them—and they are likely right in that. They therefore consider the Order as something intended to serve them for pleasure and recreation, and which otherwise binds them to one another in the closest possible way, obliging them to provide each other with all possible assistance. 

I know of magnificent traits in this class of Masons that confirm the preference I give them over the majority of other Masons. ...liam Lilly, and that perhaps the spirit-summoner <note>original: "Geisterbanner"; one who claims the power to conjure or command spirits.</note> Gabrieli also belonged to this kind of Masons. That is true: but what are a few such exceptions among thousands? Mr. Shirley had waited nearly half a century before he found a single one among all his brothers who would indulge in his follies of finding the <term>Philosopher's Stone</term> <note>The legendary alchemical substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting eternal life.</note>. And Gabrieli had to conduct his affairs very secretly, otherwise he would certainly have drawn upon himself the hatred and contempt of all the brothers. Therefore, what portrays the <term>Order</term> <note>The Masonic fraternity.</note> in my history from a disadvantageous side does not even apply to all kinds of ordinary Masons without exception; and, if one examines it closely, it concerns partly only those who believed they could find the <term>Order of the Knights Templar</term> <note>A reference to the "Strict Observance" and other high-degree Masonic systems that claimed historical descent from the medieval military order.</note> within Masonry and sought to restore it, and partly those who, after giving up <term>chivalry</term> <note>original: "Chevalerie"; here referring to the ritual systems involving knightly titles and degrees.</note>, went off on adventures of another kind, seeking to hunt down now this, now that secret within the Order.

Things that are directly opposed to one another cannot be united. This does not make the confusion any less; it only makes it greater.

In the meantime, my dear friend, it must be so; and the more the confusions within the **Order** <note>The author uses "Order" here to refer to the broader structure of Freemasonry, which at the time was fractured into various competing rites and degrees.</note> increase and become more general, the more I recognize in them the hand of **Providence**, which keeps its eye open over that small circle of men who still belong to the true **Order**. <note>The "true Order" refers to the author's belief in a "hidden" or "inner" Masonry—a core group of spiritual seekers who remain untainted by the political and organizational squabbles of the public lodges.</note> For since the affairs of the **Order** are now becoming so public, it is for the best if such **parties** <note>original: "Partheyen." -ance <note>The text continues from the suffix "-rung" on the previous page, completing the word "Versicherung" or "assurance."</note>, the stranger also finds peace, and at least does not judge this Order poorly, though he otherwise does not know it at all. The Hund's System <term>The "Strict Observance," a Masonic rite founded by Baron von Hund (1722–1776) which claimed a secret succession from the medieval Knights Templar; original: "Hund'sche System"</term> is directly opposed to this main principle. The abolished Catholic Knightly Order of the Temple <term>The original medieval Knights Templar, suppressed by the Pope in 1312; original: "Ritterorden des Tempels"</term> is just as much against the religion of Catholics as it is against that of Protestants. It is against religion when I bind myself, under the assurances sanctified by it, to things that I am not at all capable of performing, and which—even if I were willing to commit to them—would certainly make me look ridiculous in the eyes of the whole world. long before the times of the **Strict Observance** <term>Strict Observance: A high-degree Masonic rite popular in Germany during the 1700s that claimed descent from the Knights Templar</term> exceeded the initial and proper boundaries, it was natural that that which was actually the essence and the main purpose of the **Order** <term>original: "Ordens"; here referring specifically to the inner core of Freemasonry</term> could no longer take place. If, meanwhile, this more expanded circle could not attain or possess what was not meant for it, it nevertheless retained something that was very valuable and made **Masonry** very dear to all its members; and this was the bond of **brotherly love** that most intimately united all Masons across the entire earth. I am supposed to write to you about how the **Masonic emblems** might have ended up on the **Gnostic** or **Basilidean** stones, and whether **Masonry** really has some kinship with **Gnosis**? In my opinion, it is still very much to be doubted whether the stones really are Gnostic <term>Gnostic: Referring to Gnosticism, a diverse group of early mystical religious movements that claimed secret knowledge of the divine.</term> or Basilidean <term>Basilidean: Specifically relating to Basilides, a 2nd-century teacher in Alexandria whose followers were known for using engraved gems and amulets.</term>. But whether they are or not, this much is certain: Masonry is not a Gnostic and Basilidean heresy. I do not know if it was **Saint Paul the Simple** <note>A 4th-century desert hermit and disciple of Anthony the Great, known for his extreme humility.</note>, or another saint of the **Thebaid** <term>Thebaid: A region of Upper Egypt that became a famous refuge for the first Christian monks and hermits.</term>; at any rate, another saint came to this one—

<summary>The author refutes Friedrich Nicolai's timeline of Masonic history by pointing to the older age of English lodges and expresses skepticism regarding the alleged historical link between Freemasonry and ancient Gnostic sects.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the letter from page 355, transitioning from a warning about mental discipline to a historical and structural comparison of the Order to ancient schools of philosophy.</meta>

The parallel drawn between the Order and the Pythagorean school is not unskillful. I myself once had this same thought, and I was pleased to find it in the book that you sent to me. The Masons who are not yet perfected <note>In the context of 18th-century secret societies, "perfected" refers to members who have completed the initial degrees but have not yet reached the "inner" or highest levels of knowledge.</note> can very well be compared to the exoterics, and those who have the good fortune to know all the secrets of the Order can be compared to the esoterics <note>Following the model of Pythagoras, who divided his students into the "Exoterikoi" (the outer circle who received general moral instruction) and the "Esoterikoi" (the inner circle who were taught the deeper mysteries of mathematics and philosophy).</note>. However, our Masonic exoterics have not always been satisfied with the path assigned to them.

Men have arisen among them who have abused the credulity, ignorance, good-heartedness, curiosity, and the desire of their brothers to also become esoterics; they have passed off now this, now that, as the interior <term>Interieur des Ordens: This refers to the "Inner" or higher degrees of a secret society, which were often shrouded in extra secrecy and claimed to hold the "true" purpose of the organization.</term> of the Order, and then formed their own particular factions. See there the source of all the schisms that have arisen in the Order in our times.

The truth of the Order is but one, just as light is but one.

She <note>In German, "Truth" (*die Wahrheit*) is a feminine noun; the author personifies Truth here as a guiding figure.</note> does not wrap herself in a flood of mystical, enigmatic words and ideas: for this serves no purpose, and only acts to confuse minds and steer them toward false concepts. For to those to whom she wishes to reveal herself, and by whom she can be seen, she shows herself entirely and without any veil; to them she speaks plainly, clearly, and no longer *through figures or passing voices* <note>original Latin: *per figuras aut voces transeuntes*. This refers to the transition from symbolic or allegorical teaching to direct, literal instruction.</note>; to those to whom she cannot show herself, she hides herself completely: because in this case it is better not to know her at all. Perhaps some will say that this is harsh. But this complaint is unjust. For who ever asked the Esoterics <note>The "inner circle" of initiates who possess the deeper secrets.</note> (I am sticking with the parallel) whether the number of Exoterics <note>The "outer circle" or general membership who only receive basic instruction.</note> should be increased so infinitely, as has happened? Had they been asked, they certainly would never have permitted it. Thus, they cannot accuse those whom they did not call. If, however, they restrict their desires according to the situation in which they find themselves, and are and become what they ought to be according to their <term>vocation: *Beruf*—in this context, it refers to one's appointed station or calling within the social and spiritual hierarchy of the Order.</term>, then they will also be satisfied and happy.
